From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F33.0, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Mostly I came in because I’ve been tired in a way that doesn’t really add up. I mean, I can sleep, and I’m still getting through work okay—computers and systems don’t exactly wait for you to feel energetic, you know—but before the day even gets started I feel like I’m already on low battery. It’s frustrating because it’s not like I can point to one big reason for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess “sad” is close, though I don’t always think of it that way. It’s more like this gray, flat feeling that’s been hanging around for a long time, most days. Things I used to look forward to—going fishing, messing with the tomato plants, even a church concert—now they just feel like something I’m supposed to do, like checking off a task list. Which is funny, because I used to be pretty particular about my tomatoes… but lately it’s just hard to feel much about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. I still do some of them—fishing, tending the tomatoes, going to a church concert now and then—but it doesn’t really land the same. It’s more like I’m going through the motions, checking something off because it’s what I normally do.\n\nI used to fuss over the tomato plants, you know, making sure they were staked right and all that, and I could lose an afternoon fishing without thinking about it. Lately there’s just this gray, flat feeling most days, and those things don’t feel pleasurable so much as… obligations, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything lately?\n\nPatient: Guilty? I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I can second-guess myself over small things, like whether I handled something right at work or forgot to call somebody back, but guilt isn’t really the word that jumps out.\n\nI guess my mind tends to wander to practical stuff—emails, servers, bills, whether the tomatoes need watering—more than feeling guilty about anything in particular.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. I know I’ve kind of said it already, but it’s like… I still do some of the same things, fishing and the tomatoes and that sort of thing, but the interest just isn’t really there like it used to be.\n\nFor a long time now it’s been this gray, flat feeling most days. I’ll go out to the garden and think, “Well, I should check the plants,” but it feels more like another item on a checklist than something I’m looking forward to. Same with a church concert or going fishing—things I used to enjoy, now I mostly just go through the motions.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this gray, flat feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. I’d put it at about two years and four months now, give or take. It started when I got pulled back into full operational leadership at work.\n\nI thought at first it was just the adjustment, you know—more meetings, more fires to put out, everybody needing something—but it just sort of settled in and never really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your symptoms were significantly worse than they are now?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I could point to a clear period where it was significantly worse, no. It’s more been this steady sort of dullness.\n\nI mean, some weeks feel heavier than others, especially if work is piling up, but then I get distracted thinking about whether it’s really worse or just that I’ve had too many meetings and haven’t gotten outside enough. Like last week I spent half a day dealing with some network issue, and by the time I got home I thought, “Well, of course I feel lousy.”\n\nBut in terms of a definite episode where things dropped way below how they are now… I’m not sure I’ve noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt significantly more depressed than you do now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of one, honestly. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches in life like anybody—work messes, family stuff, the usual things that make you feel worn down—but I don’t know that I’d say there was some clear time where I was more depressed than I am now.\n\nIt’s hard to compare, you know? Sometimes I think back and then I get sidetracked remembering the circumstances around it, like a bad project rollout or something, and I’m not sure if I felt worse or if the situation was just worse. But no, nothing really stands out to me as a definite period that was significantly darker than this.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I mean, yes and no. I still do things, but I don’t know if I’d call it pleasure the way I used to.\n\nLike fishing—I used to actually look forward to getting out there, checking the weather, getting the gear ready. Now I’ll go, but it’s more like, “Well, I should go fishing because that’s something I do.” Same with the tomatoes in the yard. I’ll tend to them, water them, fuss with the stakes, and then somehow it just feels like another task on the list.\n\nEven a church concert, which I used to enjoy, I’ll sit there and think, “This is nice,” but it doesn’t really land. It’s just been this gray, flat feeling most days for a long time now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy and look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really look forward to, no. I still do them, like I said—the fishing, the tomatoes, going to a church concert if there’s one on—but it feels more like I’m checking the box than getting something out of it.\n\nI’ll get the tackle together and then start thinking about how I should’ve replaced that one reel years ago, or I’ll be out by the tomato plants noticing the fence needs fixing, and pretty soon it’s just another chore. There’s been this gray, flat feeling most days for a long time, so even the things that used to have some enjoyment to them don’t really feel that way now.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your current state is mild, moderate, or severe in terms of how it affects your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’d say mild, I guess. I mean, I’m still getting the essential things done at work and around the house, so it’s not like everything has fallen apart.\n\nBut it does show up. I’ve missed some routine appointments I normally would’ve kept track of, and the garden’s gone more than I like to admit. The tomatoes used to be something I stayed on top of—my neighbor always jokes about who gets the first ripe one—but this year I’ve just let things slide.\n\nAnd I’ve backed out of some church and community things too, things people used to count on me for. So… mild, but it’s there. It’s affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find give you a sense of pleasure?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. I mean, I can tell something is supposed to be pleasant—like if I’m out fishing and the water’s calm, or I’m fussing with the tomato plants, or sitting through a church concert and everyone else seems moved by it.\n\nBut for me it’s been this… gray, flat feeling most days, for a long time now. Those things used to give me something to look forward to. Now it’s more like, “Okay, did that, cross it off.” Even with the tomatoes—I’ll start out thinking maybe it’ll feel good to be outside, and then I’m just noticing what needs trimming or fixing. So, pleasure? Not the way it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms usually present for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the day, I’d say. It’s not like every single minute I’m thinking about it, but there’s this gray, flat feeling that’s just kind of there.\n\nEven when I’m doing normal things—fishing, messing with the tomatoes, going to one of those church concerts—it’s more like I’m going through the motions. Like, I’ll be there, but it feels like checking something off a list instead of actually enjoying it. That’s been the usual most days for a long time now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the intensity of your gray, flat feeling over the past two years?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to say it jumps up and down a lot. It’s more like it’s just… been there, most days, for a long time now.\n\nLike with fishing—I used to really look forward to getting out there early, getting the gear ready, all that. Or the tomatoes, you know, checking them and tying them up right before they flop over. My neighbor used to tease me about being too particular with them. And church concerts too, I’d sit there and actually feel something from the music.\n\nNow those things mostly feel kind of flat, like I’m just doing them because they’re on the list. So the intensity maybe varies a little, but the gray feeling itself has been pretty steady.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find give you a sense of enjoyment or pleasure?\n\nPatient: I know I’m kind of repeating myself, but… not really, no. I still do some of the things that ought to feel good—fishing, checking on the tomatoes, going to a church concert now and then.\n\nFunny thing with the tomatoes, I can get out there and start noticing the stakes are crooked or a branch needs tying up, and then I’m thinking about the neighbor’s yard or how I should’ve bought better twine, and before I know it I’m not enjoying it so much as just… tending to it.\n\nIt’s been that way a long time. Most days there’s this gray, flat feeling, and the things I used to look forward to just feel like items on a checklist.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find give you a sense of pleasure or enjoyment?\n\nPatient: Not much, no. I mean, I still do things that used to be enjoyable—fishing, the tomatoes out back, church concerts when I go. But for a long time now it’s been this gray, flat feeling most days.\n\nLike with fishing, I’ll get the tackle ready and drive out there, and then I’m thinking about whether I left something on at home or if the line’s too old, and it’s just… not the same. Same with the tomatoes—I’m out there tying them up, checking the leaves, and it feels more like another chore on the list than something I’m pleased about.\n\nSo I guess I still do those things, but I don’t really get much pleasure from them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been continuously present for the entire two years and four months you mentioned earlier?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say it’s been there pretty much that whole stretch. It started when I got pulled back into full operational leadership at work, which was about two years and four months ago.\n\nI remember thinking at the time, “Well, this is temporary,” like you tell yourself when things get busy. We had all these moving pieces, people needing decisions, systems issues—anyway, I won’t get off into all that. But since then, yeah, it’s been more or less continuously there.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a time during these two years and four months when you felt noticeably worse than you do now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to a time that was clearly worse than now. It’s more like… it’s been pretty steady, I guess.\n\nThere are days that feel heavier, sure, but then I’ll get distracted by something at work or something around the house—like the sprinkler head breaking last month, that turned into a whole afternoon—and I don’t know if I’d call that “worse” in the way you mean. It’s just sort of been there. I haven’t really noticed a big drop-off where I thought, “This is much worse than before.”\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present every day for the past two years?\n\nPatient: Every day… I don’t know if I’d say I marked it on a calendar like that, but it’s been there since I got pulled back into full operational leadership at work, about two years and four months ago. \n\nI mean, that’s when it all kind of settled in. Work does that, you know—one meeting turns into six, and suddenly you’re the person everyone’s looking at again. But yes, generally speaking, it’s been going on through that whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of at least two months where you didn't feel this gray, flat feeling?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a two-month stretch where it really lifted. It’s more like this gray, flat feeling most days.\n\nI mean, I still do things—go check the tomatoes, think about fishing, maybe there’s a church concert coming up—but it feels more like I’m checking something off than actually looking forward to it. Like I’m doing the motions because that’s what I do. There might be a day here or there that’s a little lighter, but not a couple months where I’d say I felt back to normal.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
